> ​@saschahennig4941😂Governments & elites deciding what you should & should not buy is capitalism.  They are the holders of capital & enforcing your choice.  They are controlling the means of production.  That is what capitalism is all about.  The capitalist controls the means of production.

> Then the influence that the capitalist has on the potential sale of their product is elasticity of demand.  "What alternatives does the consumer have in place of your product?"    
>   
> The endpoint of capitalism is monopoly.  Capitalism by design is authoritian.  The producer gets to choose the production process.    
>   
> If there is perfect competition then the buyer gets to choose the producer/supplier (infinite suppliers) at the cost of production & distribution.  That is a free process.   
>   
> The opposite of monopoly is monopsony where there is only one customer & many suppliers.  The customer sets the price.    
>   
> If you produce knives & you buy your steel from a foundry that uses slave labour or pays their miners an unfair wage.  How is that doing nothing wrong?  Nothing wrong means if you source your inputs from ethical sources. 😂  
>   
> Capitalism by its very definition is authoritian.  That is why we have government's & government regulation. ### **@dagobertfrank4383** | 👍 6 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rkt0HQOogHE&lc=UgzDvqCqIeNq9WBbZhp4AaABAg)

Word "capital" actually comes from word "caput" - head. Head of the cattle, as cattle was the important and ancient stock of wealth. And it still Is in some of the countries. In modern times it even entitles the owners to some subsidies (in EU for example).
Other commodities that became currency/capital were for example salt (salary = money paid to mercenaries/soldiers for salt). Or fabric like linen etc. Slavic word "platit" (" to pay") comes from "plátno" (linen).  In middle ages the tribute was commonly paid by cattle.
The currency (gold, silver) became more used not only because of trade but also by the fact the rulers (palaces, temples, etc.) demanded taxes to be collected in money. Taxes "in kind" were impractical".
Bronze hoards are often found as hoards of ingots or instrumental (axes etc.).

I believe Prof underreprents the role of Livestock in the economic system: In general, as i.e. most of Europe was heavily involved, but even as means to sustain warfare over longer distances or periods of time. - The success of the people from the Steppes later in history was in parts because they were less dependent on functioning logistics for food. - It might even be argued that the collapse happened at first because livestock died en masse, which destroyed major parts of the economy. -- Rome was built on the Palatine "Livestock" hill; Feudalism received its name from a  word  that means "Vieh" in German, or "Livestock, Wealth" in English. The Greek letter Alpha derives from the Egyptian Apis, etc.

I would go as far to say the sea peoples were in fact Britons, Scoti, or early vikings. A lot of Ireland's mythology and history takes place during the bronze age and is written down in the book of invasions. Just a bit of food for thought. We know the Romans couldn't defeat the people of modern day Scotland and it stands to reason they Romans attempted a takeover of Ireland but omitted this fact from their records due to a heavy price of shame involved. The bronze age Irish were known to be expert raiders and sea folk although they would not have known themselves as Irish but a conglomerate of tribes. There was also a massive tin and copper trade center in modern day Wales which was part of the early northwestern European civilisation which we know to days as the Celtic civilisation.

### **@ProhibitionJones** | 👍 5 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rkt0HQOogHE&lc=UgxecI2TMYX1UiowFsl4AaABAg)

Calling bronze age societies capitalist is reductive at best. Broadly speaking, they were command economies that allowed private market transactions. We call these specific kinds of economic systems palace economies or temple economies. They weren't fully redistributive because some of the populations may have been allowed sources of income, but all of the bronze age societies were very much centrally-controlled and their economic systems were deeply tied to their political or religious systems.

To not mention or discuss any of that and to label it as capitalism, the direct opposite of a command economy, seems like he's creating a narrative, without regard to actual history or the definitions of words. 

Greed, corruption, self-interest, resource hoarding, these characteristics are present in all economic systems because they are aspects of human nature. Saying that capitalist societies always collapse implies one of two possibilities: there's an economic system that doesn't guarantee collapse, or all societies collapse. And the alternatives to capitalist economies or societies, command economies, have historically all collapsed. So I am curious what definition of capitalism or proto-capitalism is being used to arrive at his assertions.

### **@dsm5d723**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rkt0HQOogHE&lc=UgzH50la3S_jPc6KRul4AaABAg)

I spent some time looking at academic musings about consciousness. The idea of the P-Zombie, from David Chalmers, is salient but predictably self-denying. He posits a being objectively indistinguishable from a human being, but without "real" experience, only an imitative performance of the experience of others. Pain is the obvious case. Here's my "fleshing out" of the P-Zombie concept. 

A biological being resembling a human DOES have a nervous system. Perturbing it WILL register pain signals, but pain can localize per the work of Dr. John Sarnau. The same objective physical state can register very differently in different individuals, pain included. What Chalmers misses is a zero sum analysis of the capacity of a POV to experience sensation from OTHER POVs, or empathy. I would argue that safe, paid and respected individuals turn the dial of their OWN experience up and dial down that of others. Thus, the real P-Zombie is a self-referential techno-baby, the mass produced product of the internet. 

What I call the TwatPocalypse can also rightly be called the P-Zombie Apocalypse. People will put on a uniform and inflict the most irrational suffering on others for money. We're running a business here.

### **@ABDULRAHMAN-pu2lo**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rkt0HQOogHE&lc=Ugw7WcJgU43wx_UpxRp4AaABAg)

Prophet Muhammad was indeed ranked as the number one most influential person in history by the American astrophysicist and author Michael H. Hart in his 1978 book, The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History. 
Hart's reasoning for placing Muhammad first on the list was that he was uniquely successful on both the religious and secular levels. He founded one of the world's great religions and also became an immensely effective political and military leader who united the Arabian peninsula, something no one else had accomplished. 
In contrast, Hart noted that the development of Christianity was more heavily influenced by St. Paul than Jesus, and Jesus was not a secular leader. Due to this rare combination of religious and secular influence, Hart concluded that Muhammad had a greater overall impact on human history. 
This ranking has been widely discussed and has received both praise (particularly in the Muslim world) and criticism from different perspectives, but it remains one of the best-known rankings of historical figures by influence

Capital become concentrated in the hands of very few because natural wealth is not shared. IF we charge fees to industries proportional to environmental harm (high enough to bring impacts on the environment into line with what most people would say is acceptable), then share fee proceeds, the value of natural capital will effectively be shared to all people. Indus valley did not fit the pattern b/c they followed a plant-based diet. There was less pressure to expand, to get more resources. (Socrates described this need of cow-based economy.)

He clearly shows a political bias against capitalism and the wealthy. Wars, plunder, and resource competition were common in pre-capitalist and hunter-gatherer societies, driven by territory, revenge, honor, and reproduction—not capital.
Under capitalism, war can serve as an “economic tool” for markets or national interests, but in tribal societies, it was a tool for survival and reproduction. Fundamentally, war is always an extension of power and resources; the system only changes its form.
Capitalism channels human greed through markets and capital, but desire and envy are universal. The rich pursue through capital, the poor through resentment or revolt—at the core, it’s always “I want more.”

🇬🇧 👍🏽 November 2025

Love this guy (who wouldn't?) - but he has mixed up 'capital' with 'currency' (fiat).

Capital is: the houses, the land, the cows, the slaves.

Currency is: mobile representations of that (value).

The capital is 'real'.

Currency is conceptual - which is why it went through being represented by say salt ('Roman soldiers not worth their salt'), or 'spondooliks' (spondillicus sea shells used as currency in Greece).

The overlap however - is that all must agree on what is valuable, & to what degree it is valuable.

What's also gone wrong with Capitalism - is that the link between actual capital, & currency, was broken - the moment they started issuing 'credit' - money out of thin air, with no capital actually behind it.

When you see a sword covered in thin sheets of gold, the conclusion shouldn't be "even warriors had gold". First of all, gold is not practical for combat, so it's clearly ceremonial or decorative, and not meant for fighting. It also indicates that this was a culture that venerated warriors. The system is bound to collapse because it is dishonest. (Costs are not all represented in prices, so consumers and business planners make ill-informed decisions, and harm brings profit.)

Remedy: Charge hefty fees to industries proportional to emissions, resource extraction or habitat destruction--we could add fees charged for actual harm imposed on non-human animals, then share fee proceeds to all people. If fees are set high enough to hold impacts to levels acceptable to most people, they'll rise when the economy expands. That will create a natural feedback mechanism that moderates the expansion. Sharing fee proceeds to all will ensure that the sectors of the economy that provide essential goods and services will be insulated against vicissitudes of the business 'cycle'. People will continue to spend on basic needs if they have a natural wealth stipend, regardless of job status.

an expert about it: "Gold sickness," also known as dragon-sickness, is a term coined by J.R.R. Tolkien for the destructive greed and possessiveness that comes from being around large hoards of treasure. It is often depicted as a mind-affecting illness that causes a fierce and jealous love for wealth, leading to selfish and even violent behavior. Archaeological evidence clearly shows that some of the earliest traces of bronze metallurgy were found in the Iranian Plateau as well.

Good lectures, but global misery and poverty were widespread before capitalism ever made an appearance. Erroneous value judgments of ancient times, as if they were some sort of panacea, don’t add a lot to the context of what is going on today. A lot of the issues the professor keeps mentioning have more to do with popular belief that government officials can somehow make better decisions for society as a whole rather than individuals and their communities—as if big government is staffed with omniscient beings that can know all the needs for everyone. We can keep blaming capitalism or come to realize that as more power has been granted to governments over the last few hundred years the world has slid closer and closer into central planning and socialism. The reason people give up or “quiet quit” has nothing to do with capital or capitalism. It has more to do with giant governments granting monopolies to those with the most influence, effectively ignoring the rest of the population. You can call it oligarchy, bureaucracy, interventionism, or socialism—but it has nothing to do with capitalism or people worshipping money. Money is a tool the oligarchy uses to rig governments.
